\subsection{The determinant foundation}

Manuscript~08's Section~2 proves the determinant identity with explicit
conventions, to connect the arithmetic to the OEIS objects without
depending on an unexamined factorization. In Part~III's letters it reads
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:prior-product}
 H_{k,s}(u)=\mathsf C_k\,u^{\mathsf A_k}(1+u)^{\mathsf B_{k,s}}\mathsf R_k(u),
 \qquad
 \mathsf A_k=\tbinom{k+1}2+2c,\quad
 \mathsf B_{k,s}=k(s-1)+2c,\quad
 \mathsf C_k=\prod_{j=1}^k\binom kj^{j},
\end{equation}
which is~\eqref{mxc:eq:weighted-hankel-product} of
Theorem~\ref{mxc:thm:hankel}, with $\mathsf A_k=\sum_{j\le k}j^2$.

\begin{remark}[Manuscript~08's re-proof of Theorem~\ref{mxc:thm:hankel}; a pointer]\label{mxc:hz:rem:reproof}
[Note of the batch-98 write.] Manuscript~08's Proposition~2.1 (``Prior
maximal Hankel identity'') and its proof are a second derivation of
Theorem~\ref{mxc:thm:hankel} by the same argument as
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:hankel}, and it credits the identity to this report:
the generating function and inclusion--exclusion
\eqref{mxc:eq:gf}--\eqref{mxc:eq:ie}; the spectrum and residue weights
\eqref{mxc:eq:weighted-hankel-spectrum}; the Vandermonde factorization
\eqref{mxc:eq:weighted-hankel-vandermonde}; the root products,
discriminant and resultant
\eqref{mxc:eq:weighted-hankel-rootproducts}--\eqref{mxc:eq:weighted-hankel-resultant};
and the sign count $\sum_j(j(k-j)+\binom j2)=2c$. It is not reprinted.
Also shared with Section~\ref{mxc:sec:hankel}: the remark that the size
$\binom{k+1}2$ is maximal for the positive-index Hankel matrix at $u>0$,
that this does not assert positive definiteness of smaller minors, the
reference to the different Cassini-type determinant of
\cite[Proposition~21]{mpr2009}, and $H_{3,s}(u)=27u^{14}(1+u)^{3s+5}(1+2u)^2$.
Manuscript~08 adds the small cases
\[
 H_{1,s}(u)=u(1+u)^{s-1},\qquad
 H_{2,s}(u)=2u^5(1+u)^{2s},
\]
which explain why reduced zeros first occur at $k=3$, the example
$T_u(2,2)=u+2u^2$, and the observation that the convention $s\ge1$ is
essential because the constant polynomial part of the generating function
contributes at $n=0$ (Part~I's~\eqref{mxc:eq:weighted-hankel-zero-correction}).
The write checked $H_{1,s}$ and $H_{2,s}$ against~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:prior-product}.
\end{remark}

\section{Cyclotomic multiplicities and the degree loss}\label{mxc:hz:sec:arithmetic}

\subsection{An exact arithmetic description}

Put $\mathsf U_q(u)=(1+u)^q-u^q$. For $q\ge2$, it has exactly $q-1$ simple
roots $u(\ell/q)$, $1\le\ell<q$; $\mathsf U_1=1$. Indeed
$((1+u)/u)^q=1$, and the root of unity $1$ has no finite preimage. Its
absence is the degree cancellation in $\mathsf U_q$.

For every integer $m\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:E-exact}
 \mathsf E_m(k)=2\sum_{\substack{1\le i<j\le k\\m\mid q_{ij}}}g_{ij}.
\end{equation}
For $m\ge2$, this is the multiplicity of each primitive order-$m$ zero.
The auxiliary value $\mathsf E_1(k)=2G_k$ counts the weighted degree lost
at the excluded root of unity $1$; it does not describe a zero at a finite
point.

Let $\Phi_m(X_1,X_2)$ be the homogeneous cyclotomic polynomial
\[
 \Phi_m(X_1,X_2)=
 \prod_{\substack{1\le a\le m\\(a,m)=1}}
       (X_1-e^{2\pi ia/m}X_2).
\]
Factoring $X_1^q-X_2^q$, and using $X_1-X_2=1$ at $(X_1,X_2)=(1+u,u)$,
proves the exact identities
\begin{align}
 \mathsf R_k(u)&=\prod_{m=2}^{k-1}
          \Phi_m(1+u,u)^{\mathsf E_m(k)},\label{mxc:hz:eq:cyclotomic}\\
 \mathsf D_k&=\sum_{m=2}^{k-1}\varphi(m)\mathsf E_m(k)
     =2c-\mathsf E_1(k).\label{mxc:hz:eq:degree-cyclotomic}
\end{align}
The factors in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:cyclotomic} need not be monic: their
leading coefficients are $\Phi_m(1,1)$, which are nonzero. The degree is
nevertheless $\varphi(m)$.

Every order $2\le m\le k-1$ occurs, since the pair $(i,j)=(1,m+1)$ has
$q_{ij}=m$. Thus the number of distinct reduced zeros, ignoring
multiplicity, is exactly $\sum_{m=2}^{k-1}\varphi(m)$. This is much smaller
than $\mathsf D_k$, of cubic order, and explains why ignoring multiplicity
changes the finite distribution substantially.

For computation, it is useful to group first by the unreduced grid
denominator $q$. Write
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:W-exact}
 \mathcal W_k(q)=
 \sum_{\substack{q<b\le k\\(b,q)=1}}
       \Bigl\lfloor\frac{k}{b}\Bigr\rfloor
       \Bigl(\Bigl\lfloor\frac{k}{b}\Bigr\rfloor+1\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Then
\[
 \mathsf R_k=\prod_{q=1}^{k-1}\mathsf U_q^{\mathcal W_k(q)},\qquad
 \mathsf E_m(k)=\sum_{\substack{q\le k-1\\m\mid q}}\mathcal W_k(q).
\]
To see this, set $i=ga$, $j=g(a+q)$, where $(a,q)=1$, and sum the
multiplicity $2g$ over $1\le g\le\lfloor k/(a+q)\rfloor$.

\subsection{A primitive-lattice estimate with uniform error}

\begin{lemma}\label{mxc:hz:lem:primitive-congruence}
For $X\ge1$ and any integer $m\ge1$, let
\[
 \mathsf P_m(X)=\#\{1\le a<b\le X:(a,b)=1,\ m\mid b-a\}.
\]
Uniformly in $m$,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:primitive-congruence}
 \mathsf P_m(X)=\frac{X^2}{2\zeta(2)\,\psi(m)}+O(X\log(2X)).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Without the coprimality condition, let
\[
 \mathsf P^\ast_\ell(Y)=\#\{1\le a<b\le Y:\ell\mid b-a\}.
\]
Writing the difference as $\ell h$ gives
\[
 \mathsf P^\ast_\ell(Y)=\sum_{\ell h<\lfloor Y\rfloor}
             (\lfloor Y\rfloor-\ell h)
          =\frac{Y^2}{2\ell}+O(Y),
\]
with an absolute error constant for all $\ell$, including $\ell>Y$.
M\"obius inversion now gives
\begin{align*}
 \mathsf P_m(X)
 &=\sum_{d\le X}\mu(d)\,
       \mathsf P^\ast_{m/(m,d)}(X/d)\\
 &=\frac{X^2}{2m}
      \sum_{d\le X}\frac{\mu(d)(m,d)}{d^2}
      +O\Bigl(X\sum_{d\le X}\frac1d\Bigr).
\end{align*}
The infinite-sum tail costs $O(X)$, uniformly in $m$, because
$(m,d)/m\le1$. Its absolutely convergent Euler product is
\[
 \frac1m\sum_{d\ge1}\frac{\mu(d)(m,d)}{d^2}
 =\frac1{m\zeta(2)}
       \prod_{p\mid m}\frac{1-1/p}{1-1/p^2}
 =\frac1{\zeta(2)\,\psi(m)}.
\]
This proves the estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform multiplicity asymptotic]\label{mxc:hz:prop:multiplicity}
Equation~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:multiplicity-main} holds, including $m=1$
under the auxiliary convention $\mathsf E_1=2G_k$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Separate each pair by its gcd:
\[
 \mathsf E_m(k)=2\sum_{g\le k}g\,\mathsf P_m(k/g).
\]
Insert Lemma~\ref{mxc:hz:lem:primitive-congruence}. The main term is
\[
 \frac{k^2}{\zeta(2)\,\psi(m)}\sum_{g\le k}\frac1g.
\]
The total error is bounded by a constant times
\[
 k\sum_{g\le k}\log(2k/g)
 =k\{k\log(2k)-\log(k!)\}=O(k^2).
\]
Replacing the harmonic sum by $\log k+O(1)$ preserves that error,
uniformly in $m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[write]\label{mxc:hz:rem:large-m}
[Note of the batch-98 write.] The uniformity in $m$ includes the orders
$m\ge k$, at which no pair has $m\mid q_{ij}$ and $\mathsf E_m(k)=0$: there
$\psi(m)\ge m\ge k$, so the main term is at most $k\log k/\zeta(2)$ and is
itself $O(k^2)$. The statement is true there, and empty.
\end{remark}

The $m=1$ case and~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:degree-cyclotomic}
give~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:degree-asymptotic}. The underlying average-gcd
phenomenon is classical; see T\'oth \cite{hztoth} for context. The
calculation above supplies the particular congruence refinement and all
uniformity needed here.

\begin{corollary}[Macroscopic multiplicity of a fixed arithmetic zero]
\label{mxc:hz:cor:fixed-atom}
For each fixed $m\ge2$ and integer $a$ with $1\le a<m$ and $(a,m)=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:normalized-multiplicity}
 \hzrho_k(\{a/m\})
 =\frac{3}{\zeta(2)\,\psi(m)}\frac{\log k}{k}+O_m(1/k).
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:central-atom}
 \hznu_k(\{0\})=\frac1{\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}+O(1/k).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Divide~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:multiplicity-main} by
$\mathsf D_k=k^3/3+O(k^2\log k)$. The extra $O(\log^2k/k^2)$ term is
$O(1/k)$. Use $\psi(2)=3$ for the second assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}cccl@{}}
\toprule
$m$&$\psi(m)$&Coefficient of $(\log k)/k$ per zero&
Imaginary coordinates for small orders\\
\midrule
2&3&$1/\zeta(2)$&$0$\\
3&4&$3/(4\zeta(2))$&$\pm1/(2\sqrt3)$\\
4&6&$1/(2\zeta(2))$&$\pm1/2$\\
5&6&$1/(2\zeta(2))$&$-\frac12\cot(\pi a/5),\ 1\le a<5$\\
6&12&$1/(4\zeta(2))$&$\pm\sqrt3/2$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Small-order arithmetic concentrations (manuscript~08).
Multiplicity is stated per primitive zero, not as the sum over all
$\varphi(m)$ such zeros.}
\label{mxc:hz:tab:arithmetic-atoms}
\end{table}

\section{The Cauchy limit and full normalization}\label{mxc:hz:sec:bulk}

\subsection{A direct quantitative proof}

Let $\mathcal F_k(t)=\hzrho_k((0,t])$, $0\le t\le1$. For $0\le t<1$, the
$q$-grid contains $\lfloor qt\rfloor$ points among $1/q,\ldots,(q-1)/q$ at
or below $t$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:cdf-exact}
 \mathsf D_k(\mathcal F_k(t)-t)
 =\sum_{i<j}2g_{ij}\bigl(t-\{q_{ij}t\}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Since the absolute value of each bracket is at most one,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:elementary-discrepancy}
 \sup_{0\le t\le1}|\mathcal F_k(t)-t|
 \le\frac{2G_k}{\mathsf D_k}
 =\frac3{\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}+O(1/k).
\end{equation}
The identity at $t=1$ is interpreted separately: both cumulative
distribution functions equal one.

The function $Y(t)=-\frac12\cot(\pi t)$ is a strictly increasing bijection
from $(0,1)$ to $\mathbb R$. Its inverse is $t=\frac12+\pi^{-1}\arctan(2y)$,
so it sends the uniform distribution exactly to $\Cau$. It also preserves
the Kolmogorov discrepancy.
Equation~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:elementary-discrepancy} therefore proves the
weak limit and an effective, finite-$k$ bound.

The logarithm cannot be removed from the rate. Symmetry about $t=\frac12$,
with the central atom retained, gives
\[
 \mathcal F_k(1/2)-1/2=\frac{\mathsf E_2(k)}{2\mathsf D_k}
 =\frac1{2\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}+O(1/k).
\]
The exact optimal leading constant requires a more delicate argument,
given in Section~\ref{mxc:hz:sec:sharp}. In particular the central atom
supplies a useful lower bound, but it does not locate the largest
discrepancy.

\begin{remark}[The mode of convergence]\label{mxc:hz:rem:mode}
Every $\hznu_k$ is discrete, whereas $\Cau$ has a density. Their
total-variation distance, defined as the supremum over measurable events,
is therefore exactly one. Weak convergence and uniform
cumulative-distribution convergence are the appropriate statements here.
Similarly, weak convergence alone gives no convergence of the first or
higher absolute moments.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Exceptional roots and growing Hankel shifts}

By~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:prior-product}, the full degree is
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:full-degree}
 \deg H_{k,s}=\mathsf A_k+\mathsf B_{k,s}+\mathsf D_k
        =\tbinom{k+1}2+6c+k(s-1)-2G_k.
\end{equation}
The full zero measure satisfies the exact decomposition
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:full-decomposition}
 \hzmu_{k,s}
 =\frac{\mathsf A_k}{\deg H_{k,s}}\boldsymbol\delta_0
  +\frac{\mathsf B_{k,s}}{\deg H_{k,s}}\boldsymbol\delta_{-1}
  +\frac{\mathsf D_k}{\deg H_{k,s}}\hzhnu_k.
\end{equation}
For $k\ge3$, the three components have disjoint supports. For $k=1,2$,
the last component is absent; a zero exponent in an exceptional factor
simply contributes no root.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:thm:main-full}]
We have
\[
 \mathsf A_k/k^3\to1/3,\quad \mathsf D_k/k^3\to1/3,\quad
 \mathsf B_{k,s}/k^3=1/3+s/k^2+o(1).
\]
If $s/k^2\to\varkappa<\infty$, divide~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:full-decomposition}
by its total degree and use $\hzhnu_k\Longrightarrow\widehat\Cau$. This
gives~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:full-limit}. If $s/k^2\to\infty$, the coefficient
of $\boldsymbol\delta_{-1}$ tends to one, which proves the remaining
assertion.
\end{proof}

Thus the shift has no effect whatever on the reduced zero measure, but has
a genuine effect on full-degree normalization. In particular, normalizing
only the retained zeros by the original full degree would produce a
subprobability measure, with mass $1/[3(1+\varkappa)]$, rather than a
Cauchy probability measure.

\section{The first correction for smooth statistics}\label{mxc:hz:sec:smooth}

The arithmetic jumps in Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:arithmetic-atoms} coexist
with a particularly simple first correction after smooth averaging.

\begin{theorem}\label{mxc:hz:thm:smooth}
If $f$ is a twice continuously differentiable periodic function on
$\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$, and $I_f=\int_0^1f(t)\dd t$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:smooth}
 \int f\dd\hzrho_k
 =I_f+\frac3{\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}(I_f-f(0))+O_f(1/k).
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $\phi\in C_c^2(\mathbb R)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:smooth-vertical}
 \int\phi(Y)\dd\hznu_k(Y)
 =\Bigl(1+\frac3{\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}\Bigr)
       \int_{\mathbb R}\phi(y)\frac{2\dd y}{\pi(1+4y^2)}
       +O_\phi(1/k).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The Fourier series of $f$ is absolutely convergent, and
\[
 |\widehat f(h)|\le\frac{\|f''\|_{L^1}}{4\pi^2h^2},\qquad h\ne0.
\]
Summing the Fourier series over a regular $q$-grid therefore gives
\[
 \sum_{\ell=0}^{q-1}f(\ell/q)=qI_f+\mathrm{err}_q,\qquad
 |\mathrm{err}_q|\le\frac{\|f''\|_{L^1}}{12q}.
\]
After removing the term at zero and summing with the weights
$\mathcal W_k(q)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:smooth-exact-remainder}
 \mathsf D_k\int f\dd\hzrho_k
 =2cI_f-2G_kf(0)+\sum_q\mathcal W_k(q)\,\mathrm{err}_q.
\end{equation}
From~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:W-exact},
\[
 \mathcal W_k(q)\le
 \sum_{b=q+1}^k\Bigl(\frac{k^2}{b^2}+\frac{k}{b}\Bigr)
 \le\frac{2k^2}{q}.
\]
Thus the last sum in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:smooth-exact-remainder} is
$O_f(k^2)$. Divide by $\mathsf D_k$, and use
\[
 \frac{2G_k}{\mathsf D_k}=\frac3{\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}+O(1/k).
\]
This proves~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:smooth}. For $\phi\in C_c^2(\mathbb R)$, the
pullback $f(t)=\phi(-\frac12\cot(\pi t))$ is periodic $C^2$ and is zero
near $t=0,1$. Hence $f(0)=0$, and the change of variables
in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:cauchy} gives~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:smooth-vertical}.
\end{proof}

The coefficient in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:smooth} has a direct interpretation:
the regular grids would include the angle zero, but that angle corresponds
to the point at infinity under~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:mobius-map}. Removing it and
renormalizing creates the positive first bias in compactly supported
smooth statistics. A discontinuous indicator cannot be substituted into
this theorem. Its endpoint may hit a root of small order, and then the
correction changes.

\section{Arithmetic CDF corrections and the sharp constant}\label{mxc:hz:sec:sharp}

We now refine the elementary
bound~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:elementary-discrepancy}. For $0\le t<1$, write
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:exact-cdf}
 \mathsf D_k(\mathcal F_k(t)-t)=2G_kt-\mathcal S_k(t),\qquad
 \mathcal S_k(t)=\sum_{i<j}2g_{ij}\{q_{ij}t\}.
\end{equation}
The endpoint $t=1$ is treated separately. All limits below use
right-continuous CDFs unless a left limit is displayed.

\begin{theorem}[Pointwise arithmetic correction]\label{mxc:hz:ar:thm:fixed}
For every irrational $t\in(0,1)$,
\[
 \lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{k}{\log k}\bigl(\mathcal F_k(t)-t\bigr)
 =\frac3{\zeta(2)}\Bigl(t-\frac12\Bigr).
\]
For a reduced rational $t=r/m\in(0,1)$,
\[
 \lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{k}{\log k}\bigl(\mathcal F_k(t)-t\bigr)
 =\frac3{\zeta(2)}\Bigl(t-\frac12+\frac1{2\psi(m)}\Bigr).
\]
The corresponding left-continuous value $\mathcal F_k(t-)$ has the same
limit with a minus sign before $1/(2\psi(m))$.
\end{theorem}

The right/left distinction records the atom at the primitive root:
subtracting the formulas yields asymptotic normalized atom mass
$3\log k/(\zeta(2)\psi(m)k)$.

\begin{theorem}[Moving angles near rational points]\label{mxc:hz:ar:thm:moving}
Suppose $t_k\in[0,1)$ and $t_k\to t_0$. If $t_0$ is irrational, then
\[
 \frac{k}{\log k}\bigl(\mathcal F_k(t_k)-t_k\bigr)
 \longrightarrow\frac3{\zeta(2)}(t_0-1/2).
\]
If $t_0=r/m\in[0,1]$ is rational in lowest terms, assume
$\delta_k=t_k-t_0\ne0$ has an eventually constant sign $\varsigma$, and
\[
 \alpha=\lim_{k\to\infty}
 \min\Bigl\{1,\frac{\log(1/|\delta_k|)}{\log k}\Bigr\}
 \quad\text{exists}.
\]
Then $0\le\alpha\le1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:moving}
 \frac{k}{\log k}\bigl(\mathcal F_k(t_k)-t_k\bigr)
 \longrightarrow
 \frac3{\zeta(2)}\Bigl(t_0-\frac12+\frac{\varsigma\alpha}{2\psi(m)}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
If $t_k=t_0<1$ identically, the formula holds with $\varsigma=+1$,
$\alpha=1$. Statements with eventual equality have the same meaning. For
the endpoints $0=0/1$ and $1=1/1$, the permitted signs are, respectively,
$+1$ and $-1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}[Sharp Kolmogorov constant]\label{mxc:hz:ar:cor:sharp}
\[
 \sup_{0\le t\le1}|\mathcal F_k(t)-t|
 \sim\frac{3}{2\zeta(2)}\frac{\log k}{k}
 =\frac9{\pi^2}\frac{\log k}{k}.
\]
The same constant holds after the monotone change of coordinate
$y=-\frac12\cot(\pi t)$, relative to the Cauchy distribution of scale
$\frac12$.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{An exact uniform reduction}

Write $\mathrm H_N=\sum_{q\le N}q^{-1}$ and
$\mathsf Q_N(\theta)=\sum_{q\le N}\{q\theta\}/q$. All $O(1)$ constants in
the following lemma are absolute and uniform in the real argument $t$.

\begin{lemma}\label{mxc:hz:ar:lem:reduction}
\begin{align}
 2G_k&=\frac{k^2}{\zeta(2)}\log k+O(k^2),\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:g}\\
 \mathsf D_k&=\frac{k^3}{3}+O(k^2\log k),\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:d}\\
 \frac{\mathcal S_k(t)}{k^2}
 &=\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(d)}{d^2}\mathsf Q_k(dt)+O(1).
 \label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:uniform-reduction}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $i=ga$, $j=gb$ with $(a,b)=1$. With $q=b-a$, coprimality is
equivalent to $(q,b)=1$. Consequently
\[
 \mathcal S_k(t)=\sum_{b=2}^k h_b(h_b+1)\mathsf J_b(t),\quad
 h_b=\lfloor k/b\rfloor,\quad
 \mathsf J_b(t)=\sum_{\substack{1\le q<b\\(q,b)=1}}\{qt\}.
\]
Since $h_b(h_b+1)=(k/b)^2+O(k/b)$ and $0\le\mathsf J_b\le b$, this gives
\[
 \frac{\mathcal S_k(t)}{k^2}=\sum_{b\le k}\frac{\mathsf J_b(t)}{b^2}+O(1).
\]
M\"obius inversion, including the harmless $b=1$ term, yields
\[
 \sum_{b\le k}\frac{\mathsf J_b(t)}{b^2}
 =\sum_{d\le k}\frac{\mu(d)}{d^2}
 \mathsf K_{\lfloor k/d\rfloor}(dt),\qquad
 \mathsf K_N(\theta)=\sum_{n\le N}\frac1{n^2}\sum_{q<n}\{q\theta\}.
\]
Interchanging the finite sums and using
\[
 \sum_{n=q+1}^N\frac1{n^2}=\frac1q+O(q^{-2})+O(N^{-1})
 \qquad(1\le q<N)
\]
shows $\mathsf K_N(\theta)=\mathsf Q_N(\theta)+O(1)$ uniformly in $\theta$
and $N$. Moreover,
\[
 \bigl|\mathsf Q_k(\theta)-\mathsf Q_{\lfloor k/d\rfloor}(\theta)\bigr|
 \le O(1)+\log(2d).
\]
The convergent series $\sum_d(1+\log d)/d^2$ therefore allows us to replace
all cutoffs by $k$. Extending the outer sum to infinity changes the result
by at most $\mathrm H_k\sum_{d>k}d^{-2}=O(1)$,
proving~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:uniform-reduction}.

The gcd estimate is Proposition~\ref{mxc:hz:prop:multiplicity} at $m=1$,
and the degree estimate follows from~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:degree}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The harmonic sawtooth lemma}

This is the key step responsible for the sharp uniform constant. It uses
only approximation of a Riemann-integrable periodic function by
trigonometric upper and lower bounds, and a geometric-sum estimate. It
does not use an unproved cancellation statement for M\"obius sums.

\begin{lemma}[Harmonic sawtooth near a limiting angle]\label{mxc:hz:ar:lem:saw}
Let $\theta_k\to\theta_0$ be a real sequence. If $\theta_0$ is irrational,
then
\[
 \frac{\mathsf Q_k(\theta_k)}{\log k}\longrightarrow\frac12.
\]
Suppose instead that $\theta_0=a/b$ in lowest terms, $b\ge1$,
$\widetilde\delta_k=\theta_k-\theta_0\ne0$ has eventually constant sign
$\varsigma$, and
\[
 \widetilde\alpha=\lim_{k\to\infty}
 \min\{1,\log(1/|\widetilde\delta_k|)/\log k\}
\]
exists. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:sawlimit}
 \frac{\mathsf Q_k(\theta_k)}{\log k}
 \longrightarrow\frac12-\frac{\varsigma\widetilde\alpha}{2b}.
\end{equation}
For $\theta_k=a/b$ exactly, the limit is $1/2-1/(2b)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\overline{\mathrm B}_1(x)=\{x\}-1/2$. For every $\varepsilon>0$
there exist real trigonometric polynomials $\mathrm T^-$ and $\mathrm T^+$
with
\[
 \mathrm T^-(x)\le\overline{\mathrm B}_1(x)\le\mathrm T^+(x)\quad(x\in\mathbb R),\qquad
 \Bigl|\int_0^1\mathrm T^{\pm}(x)\,dx\Bigr|\le\varepsilon.
\]
For completeness, first choose continuous periodic lower and upper bounds
that agree with the sawtooth away from an arbitrarily small interval around
its jump; then approximate these continuous functions uniformly by
trigonometric polynomials and move the approximants down or up by their
uniform approximation error. Their integrals can be made arbitrarily close
to the sawtooth's zero integral.

Here $\|x\|=\min_{n\in\mathbb Z}|x-n|$ denotes distance to the nearest
integer. For $h\ne0$ and $\|h\theta\|>0$, a geometric sum followed by
partial summation gives
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:harm-exp}
 \Bigl|\sum_{q=M'+1}^{N}\frac{\exp(2\pi i hq\theta)}q\Bigr|
 \le\frac{C}{(M'+1)\|h\theta\|},\qquad 0\le M'<N,
\end{equation}
with an absolute constant $C$. In the irrational case, for the finitely
many nonzero frequencies in $\mathrm T^{\pm}$, each $\|h\theta_k\|$ is
bounded away from zero for all sufficiently large $k$. The polynomial
inequalities and~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:harm-exp} with $M'=0$ give
\[
 \Bigl|\sum_{q\le k}\frac{\overline{\mathrm B}_1(q\theta_k)}q\Bigr|
 \le\varepsilon \mathrm H_k+O_{\varepsilon,\theta_0}(1).
\]
Divide by $\log k$ and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.

Now suppose $\theta_0=a/b$ and $\widetilde\delta_k\ne0$. Fix
$0<\kappa<1/(4b)$, and write
\[
 \mathsf N_k=\min\{k,\lfloor \kappa/|\widetilde\delta_k|\rfloor\}.
\]
For all large $k$, $\mathsf N_k\to\infty$. For $q\le\mathsf N_k$, the
displacement $|q\widetilde\delta_k|$ is too small to cross a discontinuity
unless $b\mid q$. We therefore have the exact identity
\[
 \mathsf Q_{\mathsf N_k}(\theta_k)
 =\mathsf Q_{\mathsf N_k}(a/b)+\widetilde\delta_k\mathsf N_k
   +\mathbf 1_{\{\varsigma=-1\}}
       \sum_{\substack{q\le\mathsf N_k\\b\mid q}}\frac1q.
\]
Periodicity gives
\[
 \mathsf Q_N(a/b)=\frac{b-1}{2b}\log N+O_b(1),\qquad
 \sum_{\substack{q\le N\\b\mid q}}q^{-1}=\frac1b\log N+O_b(1).
\]
Because $|\widetilde\delta_k\mathsf N_k|\le\kappa$, it follows that
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:ar:eq:low-harmonic}
 \sum_{q\le\mathsf N_k}\frac{\overline{\mathrm B}_1(q\theta_k)}q
 =-\frac{\varsigma}{2b}\log\mathsf N_k+O_b(1).
\end{equation}

It remains to control $\mathsf N_k<q\le k$, if this interval is nonempty.
For every fixed nonzero integer $h$, if $b\nmid h$, then $\|h\theta_k\|$
stays bounded away from zero. If $b\mid h$, then eventually
$\|h\theta_k\|=|h\widetilde\delta_k|$ and
$(\mathsf N_k+1)|\widetilde\delta_k|>\kappa$.
Hence~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:harm-exp} bounds every nonconstant Fourier term
over the high interval by a constant depending on $h,b,\kappa$,
independently of $k$. Apply the polynomial bounds again to obtain
\[
 \Bigl|\sum_{q=\mathsf N_k+1}^k\frac{\overline{\mathrm B}_1(q\theta_k)}q\Bigr|
 \le\varepsilon(\mathrm H_k-\mathrm H_{\mathsf N_k})+O_{\varepsilon,b,\kappa}(1).
\]
Consequently this high sum divided by $\log k$ tends to zero, by letting
$\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Finally $\log\mathsf N_k/\log k\to\widetilde\alpha$,
so~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:low-harmonic} proves~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:sawlimit}.
The exactly rational case follows directly from the periodicity
computation.
\end{proof}

[Note of the batch-98 write: manuscript~08 writes the lower summation limit
in~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:harm-exp} as $M$; it is printed as $M'$ here because
$M$ is the zero-row count of Part~I.]

\subsection{Proofs of the arithmetic and sharp uniform results}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:thm:moving}]
If $t_0$ is irrational, every $dt_0$ is irrational. By
Lemma~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:lem:saw}, $\mathsf Q_k(dt_k)/\log k\to1/2$ for every
fixed $d$. Since $0\le\mathsf Q_k\le\mathrm H_k$ and $\sum d^{-2}<\infty$,
dominated convergence in~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:uniform-reduction} gives
\[
 \frac{\mathcal S_k(t_k)}{k^2\log k}\longrightarrow\frac1{2\zeta(2)}.
\]
If $t_0=r/m$ is reduced, then $dt_0$ has reduced denominator $m/(d,m)$.
The sign of $d\delta_k$ is $\varsigma$, and replacing $\delta_k$ by
$d\delta_k$ does not change $\alpha$. Thus dominated convergence gives
\[
 \frac{\mathcal S_k(t_k)}{k^2\log k}
 \longrightarrow\frac1{2\zeta(2)}
 -\frac{\varsigma\alpha}{2m}\sum_{d\ge1}\frac{\mu(d)(d,m)}{d^2}.
\]
The absolutely convergent Euler product evaluates to
\[
 \sum_{d\ge1}\frac{\mu(d)(d,m)}{d^2}
 =\prod_{p\mid m}(1-1/p)\prod_{p\nmid m}(1-1/p^2)
 =\frac1{\zeta(2)}\prod_{p\mid m}\frac p{p+1}
 =\frac m{\zeta(2)\psi(m)}.
\]
The same calculation with the exact rational case of
Lemma~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:lem:saw} treats $\delta_k=0$ identically. Combining
these limits with~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:exact-cdf}, \eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:g}
and~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:d} proves all statements.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:thm:fixed}]
The right-continuous formulas follow from
Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:thm:moving}. The root multiset is invariant under
$t\mapsto1-t$, so $\mathcal F_k(t-)=1-\mathcal F_k(1-t)$ for $0<t<1$. Apply
the right-continuous formula at $1-t$, whose reduced denominator is again
$m$, to obtain the left formula. Alternatively it follows by counting the
multiples of $m$ in the exact fractional-part identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:cor:sharp}]
For the lower bound, fix any irrational $t\in(0,1/2)$ and use
Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:thm:fixed}; then let this fixed $t$ decrease to
zero.

For the upper bound it suffices to examine an arbitrary subsequence of
angles $t_k\in[0,1]$ at which the scaled absolute discrepancies approach
their limsup. Terms with $t_k=1$ have discrepancy zero and may be
discarded. After taking a subsequence, compactness gives $t_k\to t_0$. If
$t_0$ is irrational, the absolute limit from
Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:ar:thm:moving} is strictly below $3/(2\zeta(2))$. If
$t_0=r/m\in(0,1)$ is reduced, pass to a further subsequence on which
either $t_k=t_0$ or $\delta_k$ has fixed sign and the truncated exponent
has a limit $\alpha\in[0,1]$. The absolute limit is bounded by
\[
 \frac3{\zeta(2)}\Bigl(\Bigl|\frac rm-\frac12\Bigr|+\frac1{2\psi(m)}\Bigr)
 \le\frac3{\zeta(2)}\Bigl(\frac12-\frac1m+\frac1{2m}\Bigr)
 <\frac3{2\zeta(2)}.
\]
At $t_0=0$, the possible bracket in~\eqref{mxc:hz:ar:eq:moving} is
$-1/2+\alpha/2\in[-1/2,0]$; exact $t_k=0$ gives zero. At $t_0=1$, it is
$1/2-\alpha/2\in[0,1/2]$. These exhaust all subsequences and prove the
upper bound. The coordinate map to the vertical line is strictly
increasing, so it preserves Kolmogorov distance.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Interpretation and useful examples}

At $t=1/2$, the right-continuous correction is
$3/(2\zeta(2)\psi(2))=1/(2\zeta(2))$, and the left correction is its
negative. For $t_k=t_0+k^{-\alpha}$ with rational $t_0\in(0,1)$ and
$0<\alpha<1$, the rational correction is $\alpha$ times its exact
right-continuous size. Approaching from below changes its sign. If
$t_k=k^{-\alpha}$ with $0<\alpha<1$, then
\[
 \frac{k}{\log k}(\mathcal F_k(t_k)-t_k)
 \longrightarrow-\frac{3(1-\alpha)}{2\zeta(2)}.
\]
If $t_k=1/\log k$, the limit is $-3/(2\zeta(2))$, explicitly realizing the
sharp constant along a moving endpoint sequence. The sharp constant
therefore reflects a logarithmic range of angles approaching an endpoint,
rather than a single fixed rational atom.

\section{The arithmetic law at the extreme scale}\label{mxc:hz:sec:edge}

The Cauchy law has unbounded support. In contrast, the largest imaginary
coordinate of a finite polynomial is approximately $k/(2\pi)$. To describe
this range one must both rescale the coordinate and compensate for the
small proportion of zeros there.

\subsection{An exact tail count}

For $y>0$, put
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:beta}
 \Omega(y)=\frac{\pi}{\arctan(1/(2y))}>2,\qquad
 \mathsf Z_k(y)=\mathsf D_k\,\hznu_k((y,\infty)).
\end{equation}
A positive ordinate in a $q$-grid is $\frac12\cot(\pi r/q)$, $1\le r<q/2$.
The condition that it exceed $y$ is exactly $q>\Omega(y)r$. For real
$K>0$ and $Q\ge0$, define
\[
 \mathcal P(K,Q)=
 \#\{(a,q)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2:(a,q)=1,\ a+q\le K,\ q>Q\}.
\]
Using the representation $i=ga$, $j=g(a+q)$, we obtain the exact formula
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:tail-exact}
 \mathsf Z_k(y)=2\sum_{g,r\ge1}g\,\mathcal P(k/g,\Omega(y)r).
\end{equation}
Every nonzero term satisfies $gr<k/\Omega(y)$, so this sum is finite. The
strict inequality is important at a threshold that is itself a zero.

\begin{lemma}[Primitive points in a truncated triangle]\label{mxc:hz:lem:triangle}
Uniformly for $K\ge1$, $Q\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:triangle}
 \mathcal P(K,Q)=\frac{(K-Q)_+^2}{2\zeta(2)}+O(K\log(2K)),
\end{equation}
with an absolute implied constant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $Q\ge K$, both the count and the main term vanish. Otherwise let
$\mathcal P^\ast(K,Q)$ be the count without coprimality. Summing the
possible positive values of $a$ for each $q>Q$, and comparing the linear
function $K-v$ with its integral, gives
\[
 \mathcal P^\ast(K,Q)=\frac12(K-Q)^2+O(K+1)
\]
uniformly in $Q$. M\"obius inversion gives
\[
 \mathcal P(K,Q)=\sum_{d\le K}\mu(d)\mathcal P^\ast(K/d,Q/d).
\]
Its main term is $\frac12(K-Q)^2\sum_{d\le K}\mu(d)/d^2$, and its error is
$O(\sum_{d\le K}(K/d+1))=O(K\log(2K))$. Extending the main sum to infinity
costs only $O(K)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The edge tail, its density, and its cutoff}

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative edge law]\label{mxc:hz:thm:edge}
Let $\Xi$ be defined in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:edge-main}. For every fixed
$\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:edge-uniform}
 \sup_{x\ge\varepsilon}
 \bigl|k\hznu_k((kx,\infty))-\Xi(x)\bigr|
 =O_\varepsilon(\log k/k).
\end{equation}
The same assertion holds for the two-sided tail with limit $2\Xi(x)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Uniformly for $x\ge\varepsilon$,
\[
 \Omega(kx)=2\pi kx+\frac{\pi}{6kx}+O_\varepsilon(k^{-3}),
 \qquad \Omega(kx)>2\pi kx.
\]
Thus all nonzero terms in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:tail-exact} belong to the fixed
finite set $gr<1/(2\pi\varepsilon)$. Apply
Lemma~\ref{mxc:hz:lem:triangle}; replacing $\Omega(kx)$ by $2\pi kx$ in the
squared areas costs only $O_\varepsilon(1)$. We obtain
\begin{align}
 \mathsf Z_k(kx)
 &=\frac1{\zeta(2)}\sum_{g,r\ge1}
       g\Bigl(\frac{k}{g}-2\pi kxr\Bigr)_+^2
       +O_\varepsilon(k\log k)\notag\\
 &=\frac{k^2}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{g,r\ge1}\frac1g(1-2\pi xgr)_+^2
       +O_\varepsilon(k\log k)\notag\\
 &=\frac{k^2}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{n\ge1}\sigma_{-1}(n)(1-2\pi xn)_+^2
       +O_\varepsilon(k\log k).\label{mxc:hz:eq:edge-lattice}
\end{align}
At a breakpoint $2\pi xgr=1$, the actual term is zero: $\Omega(kx)>2\pi kx$
and a contributing $q$ would have to be both larger than $k/g$ and smaller
than $k/g$. The area term is zero as well. Hence the proof does not
exclude breakpoints. Divide~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:edge-lattice} by
$\mathsf D_k=k^3/3+O(k^2\log k)$, and multiply by $k$. Symmetry proves the
two-sided statement.
\end{proof}

On $(0,\infty)$, $\Xi$ is decreasing, convex, continuously differentiable,
and piecewise quadratic. Its negative derivative is
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:edge-density}
 -\Xi'(x)=\frac{12\pi}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{n\ge1}\sigma_1(n)(1-2\pi nx)_+,
 \qquad \sigma_1(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}d.
\end{equation}
Thus the locally finite measures $\nu^{\mathrm e}_k(A)=k\,\hznu_k(kA)$ on
$(0,\infty)$ converge vaguely to $-\Xi'(x)\dd x$. This follows first on
intervals bounded away from zero by subtracting the tail limits, and then
for compactly supported continuous functions by step-function
approximation. The limit is not a probability measure: its mass diverges
toward the origin.

At $x_n=1/(2\pi n)$, the jump in the second derivative is
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:edge-jump}
 \Xi''(x_n^-)-\Xi''(x_n^+)=\frac{24\pi^2}{\zeta(2)}n^2\sigma_{-1}(n).
\end{equation}
Divisor sums are therefore recoverable from the slope changes of the edge
density. On the outermost interval,
\[
 \frac1{4\pi}\le x\le\frac1{2\pi},
 \qquad
 \Xi(x)=\frac3{\zeta(2)}(1-2\pi x)^2.
\]

\begin{proposition}[Exact extreme zeros]\label{mxc:hz:prop:extreme}
For $k\ge4$, the two zeros of largest absolute imaginary coordinate are
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:extreme}
 -\frac12\ \pm\ \frac{i}{2}\cot\frac{\pi}{k-1},
\end{equation}
each of algebraic multiplicity exactly two. In particular,
\[
 \max|\operatorname{Im}u|<\frac{k}{2\pi},\qquad
 \frac{\max|\operatorname{Im}u|}{k}\longrightarrow\frac1{2\pi}.
\]
For $k=3$, the only reduced zero is $-1/2$, with multiplicity two.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every grid has $q=(j-i)/g_{ij}\le k-1$. The least positive angle entering a
positive cotangent is $\pi/(k-1)$, attained only at $(i,j)=(1,k)$, $r=1$.
That pair has $g_{ij}=1$; no other grid can produce this same extreme
angle. The bound follows from $\cot z<1/z$ for $0<z<\pi/2$.
\end{proof}

The edge theorem describes a collection of order $k^2$ zeros with
multiplicity, out of a total of order $k^3$. It does not describe a
limiting process for the finitely many largest individual roots. The
latter question is finer than~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:edge-uniform}.

\section{Matching the edge to the Cauchy tail}\label{mxc:hz:sec:matching}

\subsection{A divisor-sum expansion with its constant}

\begin{theorem}\label{mxc:hz:thm:edge-small}
As $x\downarrow0$,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:edge-small}
 \Xi(x)=\frac1{2\pi x}
 -\frac3{2\zeta(2)}\log\frac1x
 +\frac3{2\zeta(2)}\Bigl(\frac32-\gE\Bigr)
 +O\Bigl(x\log\frac1x\Bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put
\[
 \mathcal H(t)=\sum_{n\ge1}\sigma_{-1}(n)(1-tn)_+^2,
 \quad X=1/t,\quad
 \mathrm B_3(v)=v^3-\frac32v^2+\frac12v.
\]
Regroup the divisors as $n=d\ell$. The elementary finite-sum identity
\[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^{\lfloor v\rfloor}(v-\ell)^2
 =\frac{\mathrm B_3(v)-\mathrm B_3(\{v\})}{3}
\]
then gives the exact equality
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:bernoulli-exact}
 \mathcal H(1/X)
 =\frac X3\sum_{d\le X}\frac1{d^2}
  -\frac12\sum_{d\le X}\frac1d+\frac{\lfloor X\rfloor}{6X}
  -\frac1{3X^2}\sum_{d\le X}d\,\mathrm B_3(\{X/d\}).
\end{equation}

Define $\Upsilon(v)=v\,\mathrm B_3(\{1/v\})$ for $v>0$, with
$\Upsilon(0)=0$. The periodic extension of $\mathrm B_3$ and its first
derivative agree at integer endpoints. Thus $\Upsilon$ is continuous,
$|\Upsilon(v)|\le Cv$, and away from zero $|\Upsilon'(v)|\le C(1+1/v)$.
Splitting off $[0,1/X]$ and bounding the remaining Riemann-sum error by the
integral of $|\Upsilon'|/X$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:bernoulli-riemann}
 \frac1{X^2}\sum_{d\le X}d\,\mathrm B_3(\{X/d\})
 =\mathcal I_3+O(\log X/X),\qquad \mathcal I_3=\int_0^1\Upsilon(v)\dd v.
\end{equation}
Here
\[
 \mathcal I_3=\int_1^\infty\frac{\mathrm B_3(\{v\})}{v^3}\dd v
   =\frac32\log(2\pi)-\frac{11}{4}.
\]
For clarity, the constant can be evaluated without a contour argument. Two
integrations by parts using the periodic Bernoulli polynomials give
\[
 \mathcal I_3=\frac14+3\int_1^\infty\frac{\{v\}-1/2}{v}\dd v.
\]
The last integral converges by the zero mean of its periodic numerator. Its
integral through $N+1$ is
\[
 N-(N+1/2)\log(N+1)+\log(N!),
\]
which tends to $\frac12\log(2\pi)-1$ by Stirling's formula.

Finally,
\[
 \frac X3\sum_{d\le X}d^{-2}=\frac{\zeta(2) X}{3}-\frac13+O(X^{-1}),\quad
 \sum_{d\le X}d^{-1}=\log X+\gE+O(X^{-1}),\quad
 \frac{\lfloor X\rfloor}{6X}=\frac16+O(X^{-1}).
\]
Insert these and~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:bernoulli-riemann}
into~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:bernoulli-exact}. The result is
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:H-small}
 \mathcal H(t)=\frac{\zeta(2)}{3t}-\frac12\log\frac1t
       +\frac34-\frac{\gE}2-\frac12\log(2\pi)
       +O\Bigl(t\log\frac1t\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Since $\Xi(x)=3\mathcal H(2\pi x)/\zeta(2)$, the $\log(2\pi)$ terms cancel,
giving~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:edge-small}.
\end{proof}

[Note of the batch-98 write: in the evaluation of $\mathcal I_3$, $N$ is a
running integer of that display only, not the diagonal index of Part~I.]

The leading term matches the Cauchy tail. The next term has exactly the
scale that appears in the global discrepancy theorem. The remainder
in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:edge-small} has not been differentiated; the density
formula~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:edge-density} follows directly from the exact
finite sum.

\subsection{One formula covering the entire intermediate range}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform transition formula]\label{mxc:hz:thm:transition}
For $y>0$, set $\mathfrak m=k/\Omega(y)$. If $\mathfrak m\le1$, both
$\mathsf Z_k(y)$ and $\mathcal H(1/\mathfrak m)$ vanish. If
$\mathfrak m\ge1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:transition-count}
 \mathsf Z_k(y)=\frac{k^2}{\zeta(2)}\mathcal H(1/\mathfrak m)
       +O\bigl(k\mathfrak m\log(2\mathfrak m)\log(2k)\bigr),
\end{equation}
with an absolute implied constant. For sufficiently large $k$, its
normalized form is
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:transition}
 \hznu_k((y,\infty))
 =\frac1k\Xi\Bigl(\frac{\Omega(y)}{2\pi k}\Bigr)
  +O\Bigl(\frac{\log(2\mathfrak m)\log(2k)}{\Omega(y)k}\Bigr),
 \qquad \mathfrak m\ge1.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{mxc:hz:lem:triangle} to~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:tail-exact}
without approximating $\Omega$. The squared areas regroup exactly into
$k^2\mathcal H(\Omega/k)/\zeta(2)$. Only $gr<\mathfrak m$ contribute.
Their total error is at most
\[
 Ck\sum_{g\le\mathfrak m}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{\mathfrak m}g\Bigr\rfloor\log(2k/g)
 \le C'k\mathfrak m\log(2\mathfrak m)\log(2k).
\]
This proves~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:transition-count}. Also
$\mathcal H(1/\mathfrak m)=O(\mathfrak m)$ for $\mathfrak m\ge1$, for example
by summing its divisor expression and using $\sum d^{-2}<\infty$. The
normalization $\mathsf D_k=k^3/3+O(k^2\log k)$
gives~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:transition}; its extra normalization error is
absorbed because $\log(2\mathfrak m)$ is bounded below by $\log2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[All mesoscopic Cauchy tails]\label{mxc:hz:cor:mesoscopic}
If $y_k\to\infty$ and $y_k=o(k)$, then
\[
 \hznu_k((y_k,\infty))\sim\frac1{2\pi y_k},\qquad
 \hznu_k(\{|Y|>y_k\})\sim\frac1{\pi y_k}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Now $\Omega(y_k)\sim2\pi y_k$, so $\mathfrak m_k=k/\Omega(y_k)\to\infty$.
Equations~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:H-small} and~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:transition} imply
\[
 \hznu_k((y_k,\infty))
 =\frac1{\Omega(y_k)}
  \Bigl[1+O\Bigl(
  \frac{\log(2\mathfrak m_k)}{\mathfrak m_k}
  +\frac{\log(2\mathfrak m_k)\log(2k)}{k}\Bigr)\Bigr].
\]
Both errors tend to zero; note that $\mathfrak m_k<k/2$. Symmetry gives the
second formula.
\end{proof}

The exact effective argument in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:transition} is
$\Omega(y)/(2\pi k)$. Replacing it by $y/k$ is justified for growing $y$,
but would lose the correct Cauchy coordinate for bounded $y$. The
mesoscopic result places no extra restriction such as $y=o(k/\log k)$.

\section{The transition in absolute moments}\label{mxc:hz:sec:moments}

The zeros are symmetric in their imaginary coordinate, so every signed odd
moment is zero. Absolute moments reveal the heavy tail. For $p>0$, retain
the notation $\mathsf M_p(k)=\int|Y|^p\dd\hznu_k(Y)$.

\subsection{A uniform bound at every height}

Before applying its multiplicity, a $q$-grid has at most $2q/\Omega(y)$
points with $|Y|>y$. As $\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}q_{ij}=c$, we have the useful
exact bound
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:global-tail-bound}
 \hznu_k(\{|Y|>y\})
 \le\min\Bigl(1,\frac{4c}{\mathsf D_k\,\Omega(y)}\Bigr)
 \le\min\Bigl(1,\frac{2c}{\pi\mathsf D_ky}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
The right-hand side is bounded by $\min(1,C/y)$ for an absolute constant
and all $k\ge3$. The finitely many small $k$'s can be included after
increasing the constant, since $2c/\mathsf D_k\to1$.

\subsection{Orders below and above one}

\begin{proof}[Proof of~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:subcritical-main}]
For a nonnegative random variable, the layer-cake identity gives
\[
 \mathsf M_p(k)=p\int_0^\infty y^{p-1}\hznu_k(\{|Y|>y\})\dd y.
\]
When $0<p<1$, \eqref{mxc:hz:eq:global-tail-bound} supplies an integrable
majorant, $py^{p-1}\min(1,C/y)$. Weak convergence to the continuous Cauchy
law gives convergence of the tails at every positive $y$. Dominated
convergence proves moment convergence. Finally, the change of variables
$z=2y$ evaluates the limit:
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}|y|^p\frac{2\dd y}{\pi(1+4y^2)}
 =\frac{2^{1-p}}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\frac{z^p}{1+z^2}\dd z
 =\frac{2^{-p}}{\cos(\pi p/2)}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:supercritical-main}]
The exact cutoff in Proposition~\ref{mxc:hz:prop:extreme} and the
layer-cake identity yield
\[
 k^{1-p}\mathsf M_p(k)
 =p\int_0^{1/(2\pi)}x^{p-1}k\,\hznu_k(\{|Y|>kx\})\dd x.
\]
By~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:global-tail-bound}, the integrand is at most a constant
times $x^{p-2}$. This is integrable at zero exactly when $p>1$. The edge
theorem and dominated convergence therefore give
\begin{align*}
 \lim_{k\to\infty}k^{1-p}\mathsf M_p(k)
 &=\frac{6p}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{n\ge1}\sigma_{-1}(n)
       \int_0^{1/(2\pi n)}x^{p-1}(1-2\pi nx)^2\dd x\\
 &=\frac{12}{(2\pi)^p(p+1)(p+2)\zeta(2)}
       \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{\sigma_{-1}(n)}{n^p}\\
 &=\frac{12\zeta(p)\zeta(p+1)}{(2\pi)^p(p+1)(p+2)\zeta(2)}.
\end{align*}
Tonelli's theorem justifies the nonnegative interchange. The last
Dirichlet-series identity follows directly by writing $n=d\ell$:
\[
 \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{\sigma_{-1}(n)}{n^p}
 =\sum_{d,\ell\ge1}\frac1{d^{p+1}\ell^p}=\zeta(p+1)\zeta(p).
\]
\end{proof}

This computation shows why cutting off a continuous Cauchy density at its
largest zero would give the wrong higher-moment constants. The entire
divisor-sum edge profile contributes. For instance,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:second-moment-asymptotic}
 \mathsf M_2(k)\sim\frac{\zeta(3)}{4\pi^2}k.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The critical moment, with its constant term}

\begin{lemma}\label{mxc:hz:lem:cot-absolute}
Uniformly for integers $q\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:cot-absolute}
 \mathsf S_1(q):=\frac12\sum_{\ell=1}^{q-1}\Bigl|\cot\frac{\pi\ell}{q}\Bigr|
 =\frac q\pi(\log q+\gE-\log\pi)+O(1).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By symmetry,
\[
 \mathsf S_1(q)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lfloor(q-1)/2\rfloor}\cot(\pi\ell/q).
\]
The function $\mathsf h(v)=\cot(\pi v)-1/(\pi v)$ extends smoothly across
zero on $[0,1/2]$, and
\[
 \int_0^{1/2}\mathsf h(v)\dd v=\frac1\pi\log\frac2\pi.
\]
Subtract $q/(\pi\ell)$ in the sum. A Riemann-sum error $O(1)$, together
with the harmonic-number expansion, gives
\[
 \mathsf S_1(q)=\frac q\pi\bigl(\log(q/2)+\gE+\log(2/\pi)\bigr)+O(1).
\]
The finitely many smallest values of $q$ are absorbed in the constant.
\end{proof}

Define the two weighted sums
\[
 \mathcal S^{\mathrm{gap}}_k=\sum_{i<j}(j-i)\log(j-i),\qquad
 \mathcal S^{\gcd}_k=\sum_{i<j}(j-i)\log g_{ij}.
\]
For the first, grouping by $h=j-i$ and using a Riemann sum gives
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:gap-log}
 \mathcal S^{\mathrm{gap}}_k
 =\sum_{h=1}^{k-1}h(k-h)\log h
 =c\Bigl(\log k-\frac56\Bigr)+O(k^2\log k).
\end{equation}
The constant follows from $\int_0^1v(1-v)\log v\dd v=-5/36$ and
$\int_0^1v(1-v)\dd v=1/6$. The integrable endpoint derivative also gives
the displayed Riemann-sum error.

For the second, let $\vM(n)$ be the von Mangoldt function:
$\vM(p^a)=\log p$ for prime powers, and zero otherwise. The elementary
identity $\log n=\sum_{d\mid n}\vM(d)$ gives the exact finite formula
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:gcd-log-exact}
 \mathcal S^{\gcd}_k=\sum_{d\le k}\vM(d)\,d\binom{\lfloor k/d\rfloor+1}{3}.
\end{equation}
Since
\[
 d\binom{\lfloor k/d\rfloor+1}{3}=\frac{k^3}{6d^2}+O(k^2/d),
\]
we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:gcd-log}
 \mathcal S^{\gcd}_k=-c\frac{\zeta'(2)}{\zeta(2)}+O(k^2\log^2k).
\end{equation}
For completeness, no prime number theorem is needed for this error:
$\vM(d)\le\log d$ implies
\[
 \sum_{d\le k}\frac{\vM(d)}d=O(\log^2k),\qquad
 \sum_{d>k}\frac{\vM(d)}{d^2}=O(\log k/k).
\]
Logarithmic differentiation of the absolutely convergent Euler product at
$2$ gives $\sum_{d\ge1}\vM(d)/d^2=-\zeta'(2)/\zeta(2)$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:critical-main}]
Apply Lemma~\ref{mxc:hz:lem:cot-absolute} to each grid in the exact
product. Since $gq=j-i$,
\[
 \mathsf M_1(k)=\frac{2}{\pi\mathsf D_k}
     \bigl\{\mathcal S^{\mathrm{gap}}_k-\mathcal S^{\gcd}_k
               +(\gE-\log\pi)c\bigr\}+O(G_k/\mathsf D_k).
\]
Insert~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:gap-log} and~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:gcd-log}. Because
\[
 \mathsf D_k=2(c-G_k),\qquad G_k=O(k^2\log k),
\]
the normalization correction multiplied by $\log k$ is $O(\log^2k/k)$.
This proves
\[
 \mathsf M_1(k)=\frac{\log k+\gE-\log\pi-5/6+\zeta'(2)/\zeta(2)}{\pi}
               +O(\log^2k/k).
\]
\end{proof}

The constant after $(\log k)/\pi$ is approximately
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:critical-constant}
 \frac{\gE-\log\pi-5/6+\zeta'(2)/\zeta(2)}{\pi}
 =-0.6273278444052116\ldots.
\end{equation}
In particular, a good numerical fit to the logarithmic coefficient alone
can conceal a substantial finite constant.

\subsection{An exact second moment and a shift-dependent example}

The finite identity
\[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^{q-1}\cot^2\frac{\pi\ell}{q}=\frac{(q-1)(q-2)}3
\]
can be checked by logarithmically differentiating the finite sine product
twice: $\sum_{\ell=0}^{q-1}\csc^2(z+\pi\ell/q)=q^2\csc^2(qz)$. Letting
$z\to0$ and removing the common $z^{-2}$ term gives the sum of squared
cosecants, and subtracting $q-1$ gives the displayed cotangent sum. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:second-moment-exact}
 \mathsf M_2(k)=\frac1{6\mathsf D_k}
   \Bigl\{\sum_{i<j}\frac{(j-i)^2}{g_{ij}}-3c+2G_k\Bigr\}.
\end{equation}
This identity supplies an exact rational check
of~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:second-moment-asymptotic}, independent of numerical
root summation.

The positive absolute imaginary moments of the full zero measure have the
exact relation
\begin{equation}\label{mxc:hz:eq:full-moment}
 \int|\operatorname{Im}u|^p\dd\hzmu_{k,s}(u)
       =\frac{\mathsf D_k}{\deg H_{k,s}}\mathsf M_p(k).
\end{equation}
For $p>1$ and $s\gg k^2$, this gives
\[
 \int|\operatorname{Im}u|^p\dd\hzmu_{k,s}(u)
       \sim\frac{\mathcal M_p}{3}\frac{k^{p+1}}s.
\]
Hence $\hzmu_{k,s}\Longrightarrow\boldsymbol\delta_{-1}$ can coexist with
divergent $p$-th moments when $k^2\ll s\ll k^{p+1}$. If
$s/k^{p+1}\to\varkappa_p\in(0,\infty)$, the moment tends to
$\mathcal M_p/(3\varkappa_p)$. This is a concrete illustration of why weak
convergence and moment convergence must be kept distinct.

\section{Manuscript 08's checks and numerical illustrations}\label{mxc:hz:sec:computation}

Manuscript~08's program \path{code/07-zero-laws-reproduce.py} (delivered as
\texttt{code/reproduce.py}) regenerates the data and all four figures
without accessing the network. The proofs above do not rely on these
computations. The purpose of the checks is to test the enumerative
normalization, detect multiplicity or factor-of-two errors, and display
the rate at which the asymptotic regimes become visible.

\subsection{What is checked exactly}

The program constructs the counts allowing zero rows, $A(n,j;u)$ of
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:exact}, directly from the column recurrence
\[
 A(n,j;u)=u\sum_{h=1}^n\binom{h+j-1}{j-1}A(n-h,j;u)
 \quad(n\ge1,\ j\ge1),
\]
which is~\eqref{mxc:eq:positive-rec}, uses inclusion--exclusion to obtain
$T_u(n,k)$, and takes the determinant before comparing it
with~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:prior-product}. Thus the determinant entries are not
generated from the product being checked.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.74\textwidth}r@{}}
\toprule
Verification & Completed checks\\
\midrule
Complete polynomial determinant identities in $\mathbb Q[u]$,
 $1\le k\le4,\ 1\le s\le3$&12\\
Rational specializations, including exceptional and central roots&96\\
Exact nonreal algebraic-root specializations&3\\
Degree and primitive-multiplicity identities&91\\
Independent root merging and exact CDF comparisons&18\\
Independent direct and aggregated tail counts&20\\
Finite cotangent identities at 70-digit precision&78\\
CDF maxima over complete supports, through $k=2048$&18\\
Independent high-precision absolute first-moment checks&2\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Manuscript~08's checks. All listed checks passed. High-precision
and tail checks are distinguished from exact polynomial and rational
computations.}
\label{mxc:hz:tab:checks}
\end{table}

The empirical CDF maximum is found by checking every support angle and both
its one-sided values. These values are exact rational numbers, rather than
maxima sampled from a plotting grid. The second and fourth moments are also
computed by exact finite cotangent identities. In edge plots, the root
masses are integer counts, but comparison with transcendental thresholds
uses floating-point arithmetic. Independent high-precision tail checks use
thresholds away from atoms.

[Note of the batch-98 write: the program's record
\path{data/07-zero-laws-verification_summary.txt} also lists four checks of
PDF structure (trailer, cross-reference offsets and page count of each
figure), which the table does not count. The shipped
\path{data/07-zero-laws-pdf_validation.json} is a different record: it
reports that the four figure PDFs were opened and rendered by PyMuPDF, a
tool that is not a dependency of the program and is not shipped, so that
record cannot be regenerated from the package.]

The computed Kolmogorov distances illustrate a useful distinction: the

The movement from an order-seven to an order-eleven endpoint is compatible

not an empirical determination of an optimal remainder constant.

\begin{remark}[write; ``extended precision'']\label{mxc:hz:rem:longdouble}
[Note of the batch-98 write.] The program sums the cotangents for
Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:first-moment} and the edge-tail grid in NumPy's
\texttt{longdouble}. That type is an extended format on typical x86 Linux
builds, but on Windows it is the ordinary 64-bit double; the package does
not record the platform it was produced on. At the intake's reruns on
Windows, 16 of the 20
data rows of \path{data/07-zero-laws-first_absolute_moment.csv} and 20 of
the 4,804 data rows of \path{data/07-zero-laws-edge_tail_grid.csv} differ
from the shipped files in the last one or two of their seventeen printed
digits; every other CSV file was byte-identical. The nine-digit values of
Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:first-moment} and the plotted curves are unaffected
(the four PNG figures were pixel-identical), and the two 70-digit
cross-checks do not depend on the type. The caption's ``extended
precision'' therefore depends on the platform, and the summary's sentence
``exact CSV values are reproducible'' holds for the exact files, not for
these two floating-point ones.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Reproduction and data}

Manuscript~08's package contains its \LaTeX{} source and compiled PDF, the
four vector figures and their PNG versions, the verification program, and
the exact and numerical data used in its tables and plots. Its stated
commands are \texttt{python3 code/reproduce.py} and a \texttt{latexmk}
build of its own \texttt{.tex} file; its environment was SymPy~1.14.0,
mpmath~1.3.0, NumPy and Matplotlib~3.10.8, and its finite checks and their
scope are recorded in \path{data/07-zero-laws-verification_summary.json}
and its text companion.

[Note of the batch-98 write: in this report the program, its data and its
figures carry the prefix \texttt{07-zero-laws-}, the requirements file is
\path{data/07-zero-laws-requirements.txt}, and its build script
\path{code/07-zero-laws-build.sh} builds the delivered \texttt{.tex}, which is
not shipped; the article here is a build of the merged text. The program
writes unprefixed files into \texttt{data/} and \texttt{figures/} under the
directory above its own, so it must be rerun on a copy with the delivered
layout, as the report README describes, never in the report directory. At
the intake's rerun on such a copy (Python~3.13.5, the pinned SymPy,
mpmath, NumPy~2.3.5 and Matplotlib), it exited with status~0 after about
45\,s; nine CSV files were byte-identical, the text summary equal apart from
line endings, the JSON summary equal apart from the recorded Python version,
the four PNG figures pixel-identical and the four PDF figures identical
apart from their creation dates; for the two remaining CSV files see
Remark~\ref{mxc:hz:rem:longdouble}.]

\section{Further questions and research}\label{mxc:hz:sec:questions}

[Added 5 October 2026, batch 98.] This section collects every unproved
claim of manuscript~08: its nine research questions, with its sketches;
its explicit candidate for the correction of the fractional moments; its
speculation about the last zeros; and its three numerical observations.
Manuscript~08 states no claim that this write found wrong. Its question~8
is Part~I's Research question~5 and is printed there (see the paragraph
on it below); its question~9 overlaps
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:questions}'s ``practical order of attack'' and is
printed here with a note. Manuscript~08's questions~1--7 and~9 are
Research questions~\ref{mxc:hz:q:discrepancy}--\ref{mxc:hz:q:certificates}.

The original zero-distribution question is now answered for both
normalizations, with fixed-order multiplicities, extreme tails, and a
complete leading moment classification. The following questions ask for
finer information beyond those theorems. They are proposed research
directions for this family, not claims that every formulation has been
checked against all literature.

\begin{question}[The next term in the global discrepancy; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:discrepancy}
Determine a second asymptotic term for $\Delta^{\mathrm K}_k$ beyond
$(9/\pi^2)(\log k)/k$, and describe the denominators of maximizing support
angles.
\end{question}

The sharp-constant proof uses compactness and harmonic averaging, and does
not give a quantitative error in that constant.
Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:discrepancy} suggests why such an error would matter
in practice. A first target is a two-sided effective bound with a smaller
error than $o((\log k)/k)$. A more ambitious target is an arithmetic
description of the maximizing order and its transitions as $k$ grows. The
fixed rational endpoint formulas alone cannot settle a maximization over
growing denominators.

[Note of the batch-98 write.] \emph{Evidence, not proof:} in
Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:discrepancy} the maximizing left limit moves from
$1/7$ (at $k=64,128,256$) to $1/11$ (at $k=512,1024,2048$), and
$k\Delta^{\mathrm K}_k/\log k$ is still $0.618$ at $k=2048$ against the
limit $0.912$. Manuscript~08 calls the movement ``finite evidence, not a
theorem''. The moving-endpoint example of
Section~\ref{mxc:hz:sec:sharp}, which realizes the sharp constant along
$t_k=1/\log k$, suggests that the maximizing angles drift to the endpoints
with growing denominators; that is a heuristic, not a statement proved
here.

\begin{question}[Cyclotomic orders that grow with $k$; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:growing-order}
For which growing ranges of $m=m_k$ can~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:multiplicity-main}
be strengthened to a useful uniform relative asymptotic?
\end{question}

The present absolute error guarantees a relative equivalent for
$\psi(m)=o(\log k)$, but says little when $\psi(m)$ is comparable to or
larger than $\log k$. The exact expression~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:W-exact}
retains more information: when $m$ is a fixed fraction of $k$, only a
bounded number of unreduced denominators are multiples of $m$. An analysis
of that transition should keep the short coprimality intervals rather
than replace them by a global density.

\begin{question}[Local spacing with multiplicity weights; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:spacing}
What are the local spacing and pair-correlation laws of the distinct
zeros, and how do their multiplicity weights change those laws?
\end{question}

The distinct angles are the reduced fractions of denominator at most
$k-1$, so their unweighted spacing belongs to the classical Farey setting.
The present weights $\mathsf E_m(k)$ impose a different arithmetic
sampling. A natural first comparison is between a uniformly selected
distinct zero and a zero selected with its algebraic multiplicity. A
satisfactory result should state the coordinate used for spacing, since
the cotangent map strongly stretches the ends.

\begin{question}[The last finitely many zeros; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:last-zeros}
Describe the successive extreme ordinates and their multiplicities at a
resolution finer than $Y/k$.
\end{question}

Proposition~\ref{mxc:hz:prop:extreme} gives the largest ordinate exactly.
The edge theorem controls order $k^2$ roots, counted with multiplicity, and
does not resolve a fixed extreme rank. The next denominators near $k-1$,
together with their short coprimality counts, are an explicit finite
starting point. This problem may exhibit dependence on arithmetic features
of $k$ that are averaged out by $\Xi$. [Note of the batch-98 write: that
last sentence is manuscript~08's speculation; it is not proved or
refuted here.]

\begin{question}[Subleading fractional moments; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:fractional}
For $0<p<1$, determine the first correction to
$\mathsf M_p(k)\to2^{-p}/\cos(\pi p/2)$, uniformly on useful ranges of $p$.
\end{question}

The edge expansion suggests a term of order $k^{p-1}$. One concrete
candidate is the analytic continuation in $p$ of the coefficient
$\mathcal M_p$ in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:moment-constant}, using the continued
zeta function. This candidate is not proved here. A proof must subtract the
Cauchy tail before integrating the edge profile and control the
small-$x$ region uniformly. Merely continuing the formula established for
$p>1$ does not justify the asymptotic statement for $p<1$.

[Note of the batch-98 write.] The candidate, stated as a claim, is: for
fixed $0<p<1$,
$\mathsf M_p(k)=2^{-p}/\cos(\pi p/2)+\mathcal M_pk^{p-1}+o(k^{p-1})$ with
$\mathcal M_p$ continued analytically; it is open. What is missing is the
subtraction described above and a bound on the bulk error, which by
Theorem~\ref{mxc:hz:thm:smooth} is of order $(\log k)/k$ for smooth
compactly supported statistics, smaller than $k^{p-1}$ for every
$0<p<1$; but $|Y|^p$ is neither compactly supported nor smooth at the
origin, so that theorem does not apply to it directly.

\begin{question}[An expansion through the critical moment order; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:critical}
Can the three moment regimes be replaced by one uniform formula when
$p=1+\lambda/\log k$ and $\lambda$ remains bounded?
\end{question}

Such a formula should recover the logarithm and the constant
in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:critical-main} when $\lambda\to0$, while matching the
two noncritical regimes in an overlap region. The pole of $\zeta(p)$ in
$\mathcal M_p$ identifies the cancellation that a uniform derivation must
handle. This is a more precise objective than substituting a varying $p$
into a fixed-$p$ theorem. [Here $\lambda$ is a local parameter, not the
ratio $n/k$ of Part~I.]

\begin{question}[Smaller Hankel minors and other row models; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:minors}
Which submaximal Hankel determinants, or variants of the column
enumeration, retain a vertical-line zero law?
\end{question}

The exact resultant product is decisive for the maximal determinant.
Smaller minors contain sums of products, and their zeros need not inherit
its geometry. A first project is an exact classification for small
deficits in the matrix size, supported by symbolic examples and then by a
structural identity. Bounded entries and alternative column weights are
separate variants whose spectra must first be established. [Note of the
batch-98 write: this continues the last sentence of Part~I's Research
question~5, which asks which minors other than the maximal ones have a
definite sign, and the variants of Part~I's Research question~8 and of
Part~II's paragraph on weighted rows and restricted alphabets
(Section~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:future}).]

\paragraph{Manuscript 08's question 8: a combinatorial model for the positive product.}
Manuscript~08's eighth question asks whether the coefficients
of~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:prior-product} can be explained directly by counted
objects or a sign-reversing involution. It says itself that the question
``was already present in the repository report'': it is Part~I's Research
question~5, ``A combinatorial explanation of determinant positivity''
(Section~\ref{mxc:sec:questions}), and is not repeated here. Manuscript~08's
addition to it, which arithmetic factors a model would have to explain, is
printed there in a dated note. The zero analysis does not answer it.

\begin{question}[Effective bounds and formal certificates; manuscript~08]\label{mxc:hz:q:certificates}
Develop explicit constants and a proof-producing implementation for the
algebraic and arithmetic parts of the theory.
\end{question}

The determinant, cyclotomic multiplicities, exact degree identities, and
full-support CDF comparisons are finite objects. They offer a practical
first formalization stage. The next stage is the primitive-lattice error
with an explicit constant, followed by the CDF
bound~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:elementary-discrepancy}. The sharp constant requires
the harmonic approximation argument; the numerical verification suite does
not currently formalize that analytic step. Effective constants would also
permit a principled switch between exact data and asymptotic evaluation.
[Note of the batch-98 write: the first stage extends the first target named
by both earlier manuscripts, the reduced denominator, the resultants and
the maximal Hankel product (Section~\ref{mxc:sec:questions}, ``A practical
order of attack'', and Section~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:future}). Nothing of
Part~III is formalized, and this paragraph is a proposal, not a plan of the
repository.]

\paragraph{Observations that are not theorems.} Manuscript~08 records three
numerical observations, kept here as such:
(i)~the drift of the maximizing endpoint from $1/7$ to $1/11$
(Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:discrepancy}; Research
question~\ref{mxc:hz:q:discrepancy});
(ii)~the residual $0.473444$ of the first moment at $k=4096$ after scaling
by $k/\log^2k$, ``not an empirical determination of an optimal remainder
constant'' (Section~\ref{mxc:hz:sec:computation}); whether
$O(\log^2k/k)$ in~\eqref{mxc:hz:eq:critical-main} is the true order is
open; and
(iii)~``moment convergence in these plots is substantially faster than
convergence of the scaled global discrepancy''
(Figure~\ref{mxc:hz:fig:moments}), a finite comparison with no rate proved
for either side beyond the theorems above.

\appendix
\section{Provenance and editorial notes}\label{mxc:app:provenance}

[Added 3 October 2026, batch 85B.]

\paragraph{Sources.} The report was built from two manuscripts of batch~85
of ProveIt's incoming-reports intake, written independently on the same
subject.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Manuscript~06} (Part~I, the base): the archive
 \nolinkurl{Matrix_Compositions_Uniform_Asymptotics.zip} (21~files), main
 file \texttt{matrix\_compositions.tex} (2,078 lines, 100 labels), a
 31-page PDF titled \emph{Matrix Compositions: Exact Spectra, Hankel
 Products, and Uniform Asymptotics}, dated October~3, 2026 (Pacific time).
 Its author line and PDF metadata read ``OpenAI ChatGPT'', with ``Research
 prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov''. Its pin is the search-index commit
 \texttt{ce37e13f4aa16819c87c3ccc611362d15758f2ac} (2026-10-03, 17:05)
 \cite{proveit}. It arrived in commit \texttt{345284ac8}.
\item \emph{Manuscript~01} (Part~II): the archive
 \nolinkurl{OEIS_Matrix_Compositions_Research.zip} (26~files), main file
 \texttt{matrix\_compositions.tex} (1,561 lines, 98 labels), a 22-page PDF
 titled \emph{Matrix compositions: finite spectra, uniform asymptotics,
 and two Poisson limits}, dated 3~October 2026, author line ``Research
 report prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov''. Its pin is the tree revision
 \texttt{d68b65ea064084fb121df9bb431b21d086f7be81}; it also records the
 search-index commit \texttt{ce37e13f4} \cite{proveit01}. It arrived in
 commit \texttt{317c1ce2e}, with the other archives of batch~85; the
 placement of batch~85A (\texttt{713149ded}) held it back so that it could
 be merged with manuscript~06.
\end{itemize}
Both were placed together by commit \texttt{fa2f3e419}, which deleted both
archives from \texttt{docs/incoming}; they survive in their arrival
commits. Neither manuscript is an edition of the other: they share about
0.7\,\% and 0.5\,\% of their word 8-grams (bibliography entries and one
phrase), and their pins, code and notation differ.

\paragraph{Why manuscript~06 is the base.} On every theorem the two share,
manuscript~06 has the weaker hypotheses or the more general statement:
recurrence minimality for every column weight $u>0$ (manuscript~01:
$u=1$); a pole bound uniform in $k$, also weighted (manuscript~01: a
factor $k-1$); the coverage expansion of the whole generating function
in complex $w$ (manuscript~01: $\Pp(M=0)$ only); and the correct
attribution of the recurrence's existence to \cite[Proposition~25]{mpr2009}.
It also has more new mathematics (the Hankel determinant product, the
sharp total-variation rate, the joint Gaussian--Poisson law, the column
central limit theorem, the prescribed-column threshold). Manuscript~01 is
more general only in the kernel lemma, and that lemma only serves compact
rays, where both theorems coincide.

\paragraph{Where the merge had to choose.}
\begin{itemize}
\item Shared results are printed once, in manuscript~06's form, with a
 bracketed note naming manuscript~01's version
 (Table~\ref{mxc:tp:tab:routes}). Manuscript~01's proofs that are the same
 argument are summarized in those notes, not reprinted; its genuinely
 different devices are recorded there (Bonferroni truncation for the
 coverage probability, the tail bound $|\mathsf W_n(w)|\le e^{|w|}$, the
 direct uniqueness argument for the maximum on the circle) or printed in
 Part~II where a new result needs them.
\item Manuscript~01's $k$-dependent pole bound is printed as
 Remark~\ref{mxc:rem:uniform-ms01}, because it is sharper for $k\le4$.
\item Manuscript~01's kernel theorem is printed in full in Part~II as the
 general form of Lemma~\ref{mxc:lem:kernel}, and
 Remark~\ref{mxc:rem:kernel-untruncated} says so where the lemma stands.
 Manuscript~06's lemma is kept unchanged.
\item Manuscript~06's moments of $D$ in Corollary~\ref{mxc:cor:dense-columns}
 are kept, with a pointer to the stronger Theorem~\ref{mxc:tp:thm:defect}.
\item Manuscript~01's closed form of $\beta_1$ is printed as a note after
 Corollary~\ref{mxc:cor:784}, being the same expression; its fifty-digit
 constants and $\beta_3$ are in Part~II.
\item Manuscript~01's overstated claim about the order conjecture is
 narrowed (Section~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:novelty}), its draft OEIS text is
 printed with the correction (Section~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:oeis}), and its
 example~\eqref{mxc:eq:smallexamples} is credited to
 \cite[Remark~26]{mpr2009}.
\item Manuscript~01's research question on joint column laws is
 re-scoped as partly answered by manuscript~06; manuscript~06's Research
 question~2 is re-scoped as answered for the event $M=0$ by
 Theorem~\ref{mxc:tp:thm:couponall}.
\item Manuscript~01's inversion section is printed with a note that it is
 an instance of the transseries volume's apparatus; manuscript~01's source
 manifest (its Appendix~C) is replaced by this appendix and the report
 README, and its rebuild instructions by the README.
\item Manuscript~01's three tables (Tables~\ref{mxc:tp:tab:defect},
 \ref{mxc:tp:tab:errors} and~\ref{mxc:tp:tab:coupon}) are typeset,
 unchanged, from its shipped table bodies, the files
 \texttt{data/01-two-poisson-X.tex} with \texttt{X} one of
 \texttt{defect\_table}, \texttt{error\_table} and
 \texttt{coupon\_table}. Its fourth body, \texttt{diagonal\_table}
 (the same diagonal errors at more indices and digits), was not used by
 its article and is not used here.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{What the write changed in manuscript~06's text.}
\begin{itemize}
\item Every label gained the prefix \texttt{mxc:} (100 labels; every
 reference was updated); nothing was renamed otherwise and nothing was
 removed.
\item Added: the second author and date lines of the title, the PDF
 metadata, the editorial note after the abstract, the part heading,
 Section~\ref{mxc:sec:dictionary} with Table~\ref{mxc:tab:dictionary},
 Remarks~\ref{mxc:rem:minimal-sources}, \ref{mxc:rem:uniform-ms01}
 and~\ref{mxc:rem:kernel-untruncated}, bracketed notes marked
 ``[Added 3 October 2026, batch 85B]'' in Sections~\ref{mxc:sec:scope},
 \ref{mxc:sec:exact}, \ref{mxc:sec:poisson}, \ref{mxc:sec:dense},
 \ref{mxc:sec:numerics} and~\ref{mxc:sec:questions}, Part~II, this
 appendix, and five bibliography entries
 \cite{proveit01,pdc,tsvol,leanob,oeis784b}.
\item The figure path is now \path{figures/06-hankel-coupon_window.pdf}.
\item No delivered sentence of manuscript~06 was changed or removed. Every
 non-claim is kept: the abstract's last sentence; the scope table's
 caption; the bounded audit; ``The theorem controls the unrestricted count
 $A(n,k)$ uniformly'' and what it does not say; the moving-mean remark;
 the compactness paragraph after Corollary~\ref{mxc:cor:dense-columns};
 the prescribed-column caveat; the Hankel caveat on total positivity; the
 numerics caveats; and the caveat on the research questions.
\end{itemize}
Manuscript~01's text is restated in manuscript~06's letters
(Table~\ref{mxc:tab:dictionary}), with some proofs condensed; none of its
statements was strengthened, and every one of its non-claims is kept
(Section~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:front}, the remarks after
Theorems~\ref{mxc:tp:thm:couponall} and~\ref{mxc:tp:thm:defect},
Remark~\ref{mxc:tp:rem:floors}, Sections~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:inverse},
\ref{mxc:tp:sec:checks}, \ref{mxc:tp:sec:audit} and~\ref{mxc:tp:sec:oeis}).

\paragraph{Overloaded letters in manuscript~06.} Manuscript~06 reuses some
letters; none is renamed, and in each case the section determines the
meaning. $D$ is $\binom{k+1}2$ in Section~\ref{mxc:sec:exact}, the constant
$D_u=16b/L_u^2$ in Section~\ref{mxc:sec:uniform}, the function $D(\eta)$ in
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:poisson} and $n-Q$ in
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:dense-columns}. $d$ is $\binom{k+1}2$ and $c$ is
$\binom{k+1}3$ in Section~\ref{mxc:sec:hankel}, beside the growth
constants $d_k$, $c_k$. $b$ is $u/(1+u)$ in
Sections~\ref{mxc:sec:exact}--\ref{mxc:sec:uniform}, also $u$ in
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:hankel}, the variance $\kappa_2$ in
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:dense}, and $b_j$ are factorial moments.
$P_j(t)$ in Section~\ref{mxc:sec:hankel} and $P_j(w)$ in
Section~\ref{mxc:sec:allorders} are different polynomials; $E_n(w)$ is a
product, $E_j(\lambda)$ a saddle coefficient and $E_n=B\rho^{n-1}$ an error
budget; $r$ is $r_k=1-a_k$, the saddle, and a summation index; $v$ is $Lr/2$
in Section~\ref{mxc:sec:dense} but a tilting parameter in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{mxc:thm:joint}; and $m$ indexes $d_m$ and counts columns.

\paragraph{Checks by the intake.} The placement reran both packages on
copies: manuscript~06's suite passed with its outputs byte-identical or
equal apart from line endings and timings and its figure pixel-identical;
manuscript~01's suite passed likewise, and its checksum ledger matched
25/25. Manuscript~06's diagonal constants and manuscript~01's agree in
every printed digit, and their coverage probabilities at $k=20$, $n=60$
agree. The intake also verified Proposition~25 and Remark~26 in the
published paper, the impulse of Remark~\ref{mxc:rem:impulse} at $k=1,2$,
the instances $H_{3,s}(u)$, $H_{2,1}(1)=8$ and $H_{3,1}(1)=62208$, the
constants of Theorem~\ref{mxc:thm:uniform} and
Remark~\ref{mxc:rem:uniform-ms01}, the moments in
Corollary~\ref{mxc:cor:dense-columns}, the increment
expansion~\eqref{mxc:eq:d-incr}, the cumulant recursion at $j=3$, and
$P_3$ numerically. These checks do not replace an independent review of
the proofs.

\paragraph{Part~III (batch 98).}
[Added 5 October 2026, batch 98.] Part~III is built from manuscript~08 of
batch~98, the archive \nolinkurl{a261781_hankel_zero_laws.zip} (30~files,
a 29-page PDF, main file of 2,041 lines with 110 labels), which arrived in
commit \texttt{2172df76a} and was placed in commit \texttt{0fba5167f}. Its
pin is \texttt{d18416ec7}~\cite{hzProveIt}; this report's text did not
change between the pin and the write. Its article, PDF and delivery README are not
shipped; it has no checksum ledger. Its other 27 files are shipped
byte-identical with the prefix \texttt{07-zero-laws-}, and they use the
delivery names; its \texttt{requirements.txt} (shipped as
\path{data/07-zero-laws-requirements.txt}) is byte for byte a generic file
of an unrelated report in the Fabius tree. Manuscript~08 is a continuation
written against this report, not an edition of either earlier manuscript:
1.25\,\% of its word 8-grams occur in this report, and 0.50\,\% of this
report's in it.

\paragraph{Where the batch-98 write had to choose.}
\begin{itemize}
\item Manuscript~08's Section~2 re-derives Theorem~\ref{mxc:thm:hankel} by
 the same argument; it is printed as a pointer
 (Remark~\ref{mxc:hz:rem:reproof}), which keeps its two new small cases
 $H_{1,s}$, $H_{2,s}$ and its remarks on the convention $s\ge1$.
\item Its question~8 is Part~I's Research question~5; it is printed once,
 there, with manuscript~08's addition in a dated note, and a paragraph of

 with a note naming the formalization targets of Parts~I and~II. Its
 questions~1--7 and~9 are Research
 questions~\ref{mxc:hz:q:discrepancy}--\ref{mxc:hz:q:certificates}; the
 question counter of Part~I continues, so no existing number changes.
\item Manuscript~08's letters collide with Parts~I and~II in about thirty
 places (among them $\nu_k$, $M$, $M_p$, $D_k$, $d_k$, $c_k$, $\Delta_k$,
 $\gamma$, $\Lambda$, $L$, $\rho_k$, $\tau$ and $\sigma$). Part~III renames
 them, with sans-serif type for its colliding Latin objects and a
 superscript $\mathrm z$ on its zero measures, adopts Section~\ref{mxc:sec:hankel}'s
 unindexed $c=\binom{k+1}3$ and writes $\binom{k+1}2$ out; every rename is
 in Table~\ref{mxc:hz:tab:dictionary}. Two internal overloads of the
 manuscript are resolved by the same table: $H_N$ (harmonic numbers) beside
 $H_{k,s}$, and $\sigma$ (a sign) beside the divisor functions
 $\sigma_{\pm1}$; its lower summation limit $M$ and running integer $N$ are
 marked in notes. Its labels are printed as \texttt{mxc:hz:} followed by
 the delivered name, including the \texttt{ar:} labels of its Section~6.
\item Its figures are delivered files and keep its letters; the captions
 translate them. Its tables are retyped from its source with the renamed
 letters and unchanged numbers.
\item Its Section~12 (source audit, attribution and package contents) is
 folded into Section~\ref{mxc:hz:sec:front}, Section~\ref{mxc:hz:sec:computation}
 and this appendix; its bibliography entries for A261781, A261784 and
 Munarini--Poneti--Rinaldi are this report's \cite{oeis781,oeis784,mpr2009},

\item Following the intake's standing rule on unproved and wrong claims,
 every unproved claim of manuscript~08 stands in
 Section~\ref{mxc:hz:sec:questions}: its nine questions, its candidate for
 the correction of the fractional moments (Research
 question~\ref{mxc:hz:q:fractional}, stated there as a claim), its
 speculation that the last zeros depend on arithmetic features of $k$
 (Research question~\ref{mxc:hz:q:last-zeros}), and its three numerical

 are recorded in remarks of the write: ``extended precision'' and

\paragraph{Checks by the intake for Part~III.} The placement and the write
each reran manuscript~08's program on a copy with the delivered layout

passed, outputs as described there. The placement read every proof of

error; it re-derived by hand, among others, the Euler product

An independent mpmath computation of its own (not shipped) found

$k=500$ and $1500$ it found $\mathsf D_k=2(c-G_k)$ exactly,

$-0.62733$, $(k/\log k)(\mathcal F_k(t)-t)$ at $t=\sqrt2-1$ equal to
$-0.127$ and $-0.130$ against $-0.156$, and $k\hznu_k((kx,\infty))$ at
$x=0.1$ equal to $0.2570$ and $0.2545$ against $\Xi(0.1)=0.2520$: slow
approaches consistent with the error terms proved. The write checked

translation of every renamed formula against the delivered source. These
checks do not replace an independent review of the proofs.
